Extropy and weighted extropy measures

In this brief section, we derive the extropy and WEX measures based on the rth \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-$$\end{document}GOS from the EpWD.

The extropy for the EpWD, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 0.5,$$\end{document} is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J(Y)= & \frac{-1}{2}\int _{0}^{\infty }f^{2}_{Y}(y)dy =\frac{-1}{2}\int _{0}^{\infty }\left( \left( \frac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^{\alpha }}\right) y^{\alpha -1}e^{-2\left( \frac{y}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\left( 2-e^{-\left( \frac{y}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) \right) ^{2}dy\nonumber \\= & \frac{-\alpha }{800\lambda }\left( 225\times 4^{\frac{1}{\alpha }}-144\times 5^{\frac{1}{\alpha }}+25\times 6^{\frac{1}{\alpha }}\right) \Gamma \left( 2-\frac{1}{\alpha }\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The extropy for the EpWD based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-$$\end{document}GOSs is given by the following theorem:

Theorem 2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 0.5.$$\end{document} Furthermore, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{(r,n,m, \kappa )}$$\end{document} be the rth \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-$$\end{document}GOSs for EpWD, then the extropy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{(r,n,m, \kappa )}$$\end{document} is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J(Y_{(r,n,m, \kappa )})= & \frac{-1}{2}\int _{0}^{\infty }f^{2}_{Y(r,n,m,\kappa )}(y)dy\nonumber \\= & \sum _{w=0}^{2(r-1)}\sum _{u=0}^{A_w}(-1)^{w+u+1}\frac{C_{r-1}^{2}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2(r-1)\\ w\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}A_w\\ u\end{array}}\right) (\frac{1}{3})^{u}(\frac{3}{2})^{2+A_w}}{2\left( (m+1)^{r-1}(r-1)!\right) ^{2}} ~\frac{\alpha }{\lambda }\Gamma (2-\frac{1}{\alpha })\nonumber \\ \times & \left[ 4(4+2A_w+u)^{\frac{1}{\alpha }-2}-4(5+2A_w+u)^{\frac{1}{\alpha }-2}+(6+2A_w+u)^{\frac{1}{\alpha }-2}\right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_w=2(\gamma _r-1)+w(m+1)$$\end{document} and m and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document} are chosen such that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_w$$\end{document} is an integer.

Proof The extropy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{(r,n,m,\kappa )}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(Y_{(r,n,m,\kappa )})=-\frac{1}{2}\int _0^\infty f_{Y(r,n,m,\kappa )}^2(y)\,dy.$$\end{document} Using (1.2), we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{Y(r,n,m,\kappa )}^2(y)=\frac{C_{r-1}^2}{((r-1)!)^2} (\overline{F}_Y(y))^{2(\gamma _r-1)}f_Y^2(y)\,g_m^{2(r-1)}(F_Y(y)).$$\end{document}Expanding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$~g_m^{2(r-1)}(F_Y(y))$$\end{document} using the binomial theorem yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_m^{2(r-1)}(F_Y(y))= \frac{1}{(m+1)^{2(r-1)}} \sum _{w=0}^{2(r-1)}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2(r-1)\\ w\end{array}}\right) (-1)^w (\overline{F}_Y(y))^{w(m+1)}.$$\end{document}Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{Y(r,n,m,\kappa )}^2(y)= \frac{C_{r-1}^2}{(m+1)^{2(r-1)}((r-1)!)^2} \sum _{w=0}^{2(r-1)}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2(r-1)\\ w\end{array}}\right) (-1)^w (\overline{F}_Y(y))^{2(\gamma _r-1)+w(m+1)} f_Y^2(y).$$\end{document}Using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{F}_Y(y)=\frac{3}{2}e^{-2z}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-3z}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_Y(y)=\frac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^\alpha }y^{\alpha -1}(2e^{-2z}-e^{-3z}),$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=(y/\lambda )^\alpha ,$$\end{document} we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_Y^2(y)=\left( \frac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^\alpha }\right) ^2 y^{2(\alpha -1)}(2e^{-2z}-e^{-3z})^2.$$\end{document}Expanding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2e^{-2z}-e^{-3z})^2 = 4e^{-4z}-4e^{-5z}+e^{-6z}$$\end{document} and similarly expanding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{F}_Y(y))^{A_w}$$\end{document} via the binomial theorem (since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_w$$\end{document} is an integer), the integrand becomes a finite sum of terms of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y^{2(\alpha -1)} e^{-c (y/\lambda )^\alpha },$$\end{document} for suitable constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0.$$\end{document} Thus, each term reduces to an integral of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^\infty y^{2\alpha -2} e^{-c (y/\lambda )^\alpha }\,dy. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Using the substitution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=(y/\lambda )^\alpha ,$$\end{document} we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\infty y^{2\alpha -2} e^{-c (y/\lambda )^\alpha }\,dy=\frac{\lambda ^{2\alpha -1}}{\alpha } c^{-(2-1/\alpha )}\Gamma \!\left( 2-\frac{1}{\alpha }\right) .$$\end{document}Substituting these expressions back and summing over all indices yields the desired result.

The WEX for the EpWD is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J^{(\omega )}(Y) = \frac{-1}{2}\int _{0}^{\infty }yf^{2}_{Y}(y)dy=\frac{-1}{2}\int _{0}^{\infty }y\left( \left( \frac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^{\alpha }}\right) y^{\alpha -1}e^{-2\left( \frac{y}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\left( 2-e^{-\left( \frac{y}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) \right) ^{2}dy =\frac{-53\alpha }{400}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The WEX for the EpWD based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-$$\end{document}GOSs is given by the following theorem:

Theorem 3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{(r,n,m, \kappa )}$$\end{document} be the rth \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-$$\end{document}GOSs for EpWD, then the WEX of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{(r,n,m, \kappa )}$$\end{document} is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J^{(\omega )}(Y_{(r,n,m, \kappa )})= & \frac{-1}{2}\int _{0}^{\infty }yf^{2}_{Y(r,n,m, \kappa )}(y)dy\nonumber \\= & \sum _{w=0}^{2(r-1)}\sum _{u=0}^{A_w}(-1)^{w+u+1}\frac{\alpha C_{r-1}^{2}\left( {\begin{array}{c}2(r-1)\\ w\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}A_w\\ u\end{array}}\right) (\frac{1}{3})^{u} \left( \frac{3}{2}\right) ^{2+A_w}}{2\left( (m+1)^{r-1}(r-1)!\right) ^{2}}\nonumber \\ \times & \left[ \frac{4}{(4+2A_w+u)^{2}}-\frac{4}{(5+2A_w+u)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(6+2A_w+u)^{2}}\right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof The WEX of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_{(r,n,m,\kappa )}$$\end{document} is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}(Y_{(r,n,m,\kappa )})=-\frac{1}{2}\int _0^\infty y\, f_{Y(r,n,m,\kappa )}^2(y)\,dy.$$\end{document} Proceeding as in the proof of Theorem 3.1, we expand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{Y(r,n,m,\kappa )}^2(y)$$\end{document} and reduce the integral to a finite sum of terms of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^\infty y^{2\alpha -1} e^{-c (y/\lambda )^\alpha }\,dy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}for suitable constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0.$$\end{document} Using the substitution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=(y/\lambda )^\alpha ,$$\end{document} we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^\infty y^{2\alpha -1} e^{-c (y/\lambda )^\alpha } dy=\frac{\lambda ^{2\alpha }}{\alpha } c^{-2} \Gamma (2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma (2)=1$$\end{document}, this simplifies to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\lambda ^{2\alpha }}{\alpha } c^{-2}.$$\end{document} Compared to Theorem 3.1, where the corresponding integral involves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y^{2\alpha -2}$$\end{document} and yields a factor proportional to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^{-(2-1/\alpha )}$$\end{document}, the additional factor y increases the exponent by one, resulting in a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^{-2}$$\end{document} dependence. Substituting these expressions back into the expanded form and summing over all indices yields the desired result. The remaining algebraic steps follow directly from those in Theorem 3.1 and are therefore omitted for brevity.

Interpretation and practical relevance

The extropy and WEX measures derived above offer distinct insights into the structure of the EpWD. To illustrate their interpretation, consider the following:

Scale and units: Both J(Y) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}(Y)$$\end{document} are negative-valued for continuous distributions (since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{1}{2}\int f_Y^2(y)dy < 0$$\end{document}). Their absolute values increase with the dispersion of the distribution. For the EpWD, larger values of the shape parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document} (indicating a steeper failure rate) generally lead to smaller absolute extropy, reflecting increased concentration around the scale parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document}.

Tail sensitivity: The WEX \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}(Y)$$\end{document} is more sensitive than J(Y) to changes in the upper tail of the distribution. This is evident from the weight function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(y)=y,$$\end{document} which amplifies contributions from larger y values. In reliability terms, J(Y) summarizes overall failure time uncertainty, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}(Y)$$\end{document} emphasizes uncertainty about late failures-a critical distinction when designing maintenance schedules or warranty periods.

Comparison with entropy: For the EpWD, the differential entropy H(Y) can be derived and compared with J(Y). While H(Y) measures average unpredictability in terms of log-density, J(Y) measures concentration in terms of squared density. These two measures are complementary: a distribution can have high entropy (high unpredictability) but low absolute extropy (high concentration) if it is concentrated in regions of low density, and vice versa. The simulation results in Section Simulation study (Table 3) demonstrate that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}(Y)$$\end{document} exhibits better estimability than J(Y), with narrower confidence/credible intervals across all scenarios. This improved precision makes WEX a more reliable tool for inference in practical applications where tail behavior is of interest. 3

Practical implications: The choice between entropy, extropy, and WEX depends on the application context:Use entropy when the goal is to quantify overall uncertainty or disorder in a system.Use extropy when the focus is on distributional concentration, such as assessing how tightly failure times cluster around a typical value.Use WEX when tail events are of primary concern, as in extreme value analysis, reliability of aging systems, or environmental risk assessment.In the real data applications of Section Application of real data, the estimated extropy and WEX values (Table 8) provide complementary summaries of the fitted EpWD: J captures overall concentration, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}$$\end{document} emphasizes the uncertainty in the upper tail of failure times, offering insights that would not be available from entropy alone.

Use entropy when the goal is to quantify overall uncertainty or disorder in a system.

Use entropy when the goal is to quantify overall uncertainty or disorder in a system.

Use extropy when the focus is on distributional concentration, such as assessing how tightly failure times cluster around a typical value.

Use extropy when the focus is on distributional concentration, such as assessing how tightly failure times cluster around a typical value.

Use WEX when tail events are of primary concern, as in extreme value analysis, reliability of aging systems, or environmental risk assessment.

Use WEX when tail events are of primary concern, as in extreme value analysis, reliability of aging systems, or environmental risk assessment.

Connection to risk measures: While not the primary focus of this paper, the EpWD’s finite moments of all orders (Remark 2.2) guarantee the existence of standard risk measures such as Value-at-Risk (VaR) and Tail Value-at-Risk (TVaR). For a given probability level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in (0,1),$$\end{document} VaR\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_p$$\end{document} is simply the p-th quantile of the distribution, obtained by inverting Equation (3). In reliability engineering, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{10}$$\end{document} life-the time by which 10% of a population of units is expected to have failed-corresponds to VaR\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{0.10},$$\end{document} providing a benchmark for warranty periods and maintenance scheduling. TVaR\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_p,$$\end{document} which measures the expected loss exceeding VaR\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_p,$$\end{document} can be computed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {TVaR}_p = \frac{1}{1-p}\int _{p}^{1} \text {VaR}_u \, du,$$\end{document} offering a more conservative risk assessment by averaging failures beyond the threshold, which is particularly valuable for safety-critical applications where extreme events are of concern. The WEX \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\omega )}(Y)$$\end{document} derived in Theorem 3.2 complements these measures by quantifying uncertainty specifically in the upper tail, providing an information-theoretic perspective on extreme failure times. Together, VaR (threshold), TVaR (expected severity), and WEX (tail uncertainty) form a comprehensive toolkit for risk assessment in engineering and electronics applications.

Two different estimation methods

In this part, the MLE and Bayesian techniques are developed for the parameters of EpWD based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-$$\end{document}GOSs. Furthermore, asymptotic CIs for the model parameters are constructed using the FIM.

MLE method

Consider GOSs sample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{Y} = (y_1:=y_{(1, n,m, \kappa )}, y_2:=y_{(2, n,m, \kappa )},..., y_n:=y_{(n, n,m, \kappa ))}$$\end{document} of size n drawn from the EpWD. The MLE method is a widely used and powerful statistical technique. It provides parameter estimates with desirable asymptotic properties, such as consistency, asymptotic unbiasedness, efficiency, and normality. The MLE approach involves finding the parameter values that maximize the likelihood function, thereby yielding estimates that best explain the observed sample data. The log-likelihood function of the given sample is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ln L(\underline{Y};\alpha ,\lambda )= & n\ln \left( \frac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^{\alpha }}\right) +\left( \kappa -1\right) \ln \left( \frac{3}{2}e^{-2\left( \frac{y_{n}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-3\left( \frac{y_{n}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) \nonumber \\+ & m\sum _{j=1}^{n-1}\ln \left( \frac{3}{2}e^{-2\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-3\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) \nonumber \\ + & \sum _{j=1}^{n}\ln \gamma _j+\left( \alpha -1\right) \sum _{j=1}^{n}\ln y_{j}-2 \sum _{j=1}^{n}\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }+\sum _{j=1}^{n}\ln \left( 2-e^{-\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) .\qquad \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The normal equations are obtained by partially differentiating the log-likelihood function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ln L(\underline{Y};\alpha ,\lambda )$$\end{document} with respect to the parameter vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha , \lambda )$$\end{document} and setting the resulting expressions equal to zero. These partial derivatives are given below\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{gathered} \frac{{\partial \ln L(\underset{\raise0.3em\hbox{$\smash{\scriptscriptstyle-}$}}{Y} ;\alpha ,\lambda )}}{{\partial \alpha }} = n\left( {\frac{1}{\alpha } - \ln \lambda } \right) + \frac{{\left( {\kappa - 1} \right)3e^{{ - 2\left( {\frac{{y_{n} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} \left( {\frac{{y_{n} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } \left( {\frac{1}{2}e^{{ - \frac{1}{2}e^{{ - 3\left( {\frac{{y_{n} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} } \right)}} \hfill \\ \quad \quad \quad \quad - 2\sum\limits_{{j = 1}}^{n} {\left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } } \ln \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right) + \sum\limits_{{j = 1}}^{n} {\ln } y_{j} - \sum\limits_{{j = 1}}^{n} {\frac{{e^{{ - \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } \ln \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)}}{{2 - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} }}} \hfill \\ \quad \quad \quad \quad + m\sum\limits_{{j = 1}}^{{n - 1}} {\frac{{3e^{{ - 2\left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } \left( {\frac{1}{2}e^{{ - \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} - 1} \right)\ln \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)}}{{\left( {\frac{3}{2}e^{{ - 2\left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} - \frac{1}{2}e^{{ - 3\left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} } \right)}}} \hfill \\ \end{gathered}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{gathered} \frac{{\partial \ln L(\underset{\raise0.3em\hbox{$\smash{\scriptscriptstyle-}$}}{Y} ;\alpha ,\lambda )}}{{\partial \lambda }} = \frac{{ - n\alpha }}{\lambda } - \left( {\kappa - 1} \right)\frac{{\frac{{3\alpha y_{n} }}{{\lambda ^{2} }}e^{{ - 2\left( {\frac{{y_{n} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} \left( {\frac{{y_{n} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{{\alpha - 1}} - \sum\limits_{{j = 1}}^{n} {\frac{{\alpha y_{j} e^{{ - \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{{\alpha - 1}} }}{{\lambda ^{2} \left( {2 - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{{y_{j} }}{\lambda }} \right)^{\alpha } }} } \right)}}} \hfill \\ \quad \quad \quad \quad - m\sum\limits_{{j = 1}}^{{n - 1}} {\frac{{\frac{{3\alpha y_{j} }}{{\lambda ^{2} }}e^{{ - For implementation details, refer to Section Simulation study and Tables 2, 3, 4.

Numerical optimization procedure for MLE

This subsection provides a detailed description of the optimization procedure employed in our study, addressing practical considerations essential for reproducibility.

Optimization algorithm. We employed the Newton-Raphson method, an iterative gradient-based algorithm that updates parameter estimates according to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)} = \boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k)} - \left[ \textbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k)})\right] ^{-1} \textbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k)}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta } = (\alpha , \lambda )^\top$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta }) = \left( \frac{\partial \ln L}{\partial \alpha }, \frac{\partial \ln L}{\partial \lambda }\right) ^\top$$\end{document} is the gradient vector, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}(\boldsymbol{\theta })$$\end{document} is the Hessian matrix (the matrix of second-order partial derivatives). The Newton-Raphson method was chosen for its quadratic convergence rate near the optimum, which is particularly advantageous given the smoothness and regularity of the EpWD likelihood surface.

Initial value selection. The choice of starting values is critical for ensuring convergence of the Newton-Raphson algorithm. In our simulation study, initial values for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha , \lambda )$$\end{document} were obtained using the method of moments based on the full (uncensored) sample. Convergence criteria. The Newton-Raphson iteration was terminated when one of the following conditions was satisfied:Relative change in log-likelihood: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \ln L(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)}) - \ln L(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k)})\right| < \varepsilon _1$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _1 = 10^{-6}.$$\end{document}Gradient norm: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \textbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)})\Vert < \varepsilon _2$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _2 = 10^{-5}.$$\end{document}Maximum iterations: The algorithm was stopped after 200 iterations if convergence was not achieved, in which case the replicate was flagged for non-convergence.These criteria ensure that the estimates are sufficiently close to the true maximum while balancing computational efficiency.

Relative change in log-likelihood: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \ln L(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)}) - \ln L(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k)})\right| < \varepsilon _1$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _1 = 10^{-6}.$$\end{document}

Relative change in log-likelihood: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \ln L(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)}) - \ln L(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k)})\right| < \varepsilon _1$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _1 = 10^{-6}.$$\end{document}

Gradient norm: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \textbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)})\Vert < \varepsilon _2$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _2 = 10^{-5}.$$\end{document}

Gradient norm: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \textbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)})\Vert < \varepsilon _2$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _2 = 10^{-5}.$$\end{document}

Maximum iterations: The algorithm was stopped after 200 iterations if convergence was not achieved, in which case the replicate was flagged for non-convergence.

Maximum iterations: The algorithm was stopped after 200 iterations if convergence was not achieved, in which case the replicate was flagged for non-convergence.

Handling non-convergence and numerical instability. Despite the regularity of the EpWD, numerical instability can occasionally arise, particularly in small samples or under heavy censoring. The following measures were implemented to address such issues:Step-size adjustment: If the update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)}$$\end{document} resulted in a decrease in the log-likelihood (indicating that the full Newton step overshot the maximum), a backtracking line search was employed to reduce the step size by half until the log-likelihood increased.Hessian modification: In cases, where the Hessian matrix was not positive definite (indicating that the current point was not in a concave region), we applied a modified Cholesky decomposition to ensure that the search direction remained a descent direction.Parameter bounds: At each iteration, we enforced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha> 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda> 0$$\end{document} by projecting any negative updates back to a small positive value (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-6}$$\end{document}).Non-convergence rates in simulation. Across all simulation scenarios, the proportion of replicates where the Newton-Raphson algorithm failed to converge within 200 iterations was consistently below \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.5\%$$\end{document}. These non-convergent replicates were excluded from the summary statistics (mean squared error (MSE), mean relative error (MRE), average half length (AL), and coverage probability (CP)) to avoid bias, and a new sample was generated to maintain the target of 10, 000 valid replicates per scenario. This approach ensures that the reported performance metrics are representative of the algorithm’s behavior under typical operating conditions.

Step-size adjustment: If the update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)}$$\end{document} resulted in a decrease in the log-likelihood (indicating that the full Newton step overshot the maximum), a backtracking line search was employed to reduce the step size by half until the log-likelihood increased.

Step-size adjustment: If the update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\theta }^{(k+1)}$$\end{document} resulted in a decrease in the log-likelihood (indicating that the full Newton step overshot the maximum), a backtracking line search was employed to reduce the step size by half until the log-likelihood increased.

Hessian modification: In cases, where the Hessian matrix was not positive definite (indicating that the current point was not in a concave region), we applied a modified Cholesky decomposition to ensure that the search direction remained a descent direction.

Hessian modification: In cases, where the Hessian matrix was not positive definite (indicating that the current point was not in a concave region), we applied a modified Cholesky decomposition to ensure that the search direction remained a descent direction.

Parameter bounds: At each iteration, we enforced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha> 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda> 0$$\end{document} by projecting any negative updates back to a small positive value (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-6}$$\end{document}).

Parameter bounds: At each iteration, we enforced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha> 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda> 0$$\end{document} by projecting any negative updates back to a small positive value (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-6}$$\end{document}).

Software implementation. All optimization procedures were implemented in the R programming environment (R Core Team26,). The core Newton-Raphson algorithm was coded from scratch to maximize transparency and control, rather than relying on black-box optimizers. For verification purposes, selected scenarios were cross-checked using the optim() function with the BFGS method, which yielded nearly identical estimates (maximum relative difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$< 10^{-4}$$\end{document}), confirming the correctness of our implementation.

Bayesian estimation

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi =(\alpha , \lambda )$$\end{document} denote the shape and scale parameters of the EpWD. Assume independent gamma priors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \operatorname {Gamma}(c,d),~~\lambda \sim \operatorname {Gamma}(a,b),$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,c>0,\; b,d>0.$$\end{document} Thus, the joint prior density is13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi (\varphi ) \;=\; \frac{b^{\,a} d^{\,c}}{\Gamma (a)\Gamma (c)}\, \lambda ^{a-1} e^{-b\lambda }\, \alpha ^{c-1} e^{-d\alpha }, \qquad \alpha ,\lambda>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The hyper-parameters (a, b, c, d) can be elicited by matching the prior means and variances to the MLEs and their asymptotic variance-covariance matrix; see, for example, Dey et al.27, Gupta and Kundu28, and Hamdy and Almetwally29.

For an m-GOS sample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{Y}=(y_1:=y_{(1,n,m,\kappa )},\dots ,y_n:=y_{(n,n,m,\kappa )}),$$\end{document} the likelihood function is14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L(\underline{Y};\varphi )&=\! \Biggl [ \prod _{j=1}^{n-1} \gamma _j \Bigl \{ \bigl (\tfrac{3}{2} e^{-2(y_j/\lambda )^{\alpha }} -\tfrac{1}{2} e^{-3(y_j/\lambda )^{\alpha }}\bigr )^{m} \bigl (\tfrac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^{\alpha }}\bigr ) y_j^{\alpha -1} e^{-2(y_j/\lambda )^{\alpha }} \bigl (2-e^{-(y_j/\lambda )^{\alpha }}\bigr ) \Bigr \} \Biggr ] \nonumber \\&\quad \times \bigl (\tfrac{3}{2} e^{-2(y_n/\lambda )^{\alpha }} -\tfrac{1}{2} e^{-3(y_n/\lambda )^{\alpha }}\bigr )^{\kappa -1} \bigl (\tfrac{3\alpha }{2\lambda ^{\alpha }}\bigr ) y_n^{\alpha -1} e^{-2(y_n/\lambda )^{\alpha }} \bigl (2-e^{-(y_n/\lambda )^{\alpha }}\bigr ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Combining (13) and (14) yields the unnormalised posterior kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi (\varphi \mid \underline{Y})\;\propto \;L(\underline{Y}\,;\varphi )\, \pi (\varphi ),$$\end{document} that is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} Prior justification and elicitation

The choice of independent gamma priors for the shape parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document} and scale parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document} is motivated by several considerations. First, the gamma distribution is a natural and flexible prior for positive parameters, offering a conjugate-like structure that facilitates computation. Second, by varying its hyperparameters, the gamma prior can represent a wide range of prior beliefs, from highly informative to weakly informative or even non-informative. Specifically, the gamma prior density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (\theta ) \propto \theta ^{a-1} e^{-b\theta }$$\end{document} has mean a/b and variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a/b^2$$\end{document}, allowing the user to calibrate the prior to match prior expectations or to remain vague.

In our study (see Section Simulation study), we employ two distinct prior settings to assess the sensitivity of posterior inferences to the prior specification:Bayesian with informative priors (B.Inf): We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \text {Gamma}(2,1)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \sim \text {Gamma}(2,1)$$\end{document}. These priors have a mean of 2 and a variance of 2, providing a weak but proper prior belief centered around a reasonable positive value. This choice reflects a modest degree of prior information without dominating the likelihood.Bayesian with non- informative priors (B.Non-Inf): We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \text {Gamma}(0.001, 0.001)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \sim \text {Gamma}(0.001,$$\end{document} 0.001). This approximates an improper prior with mean 1 and a very large variance (1000), allowing the data to dominate the posterior. It serves as a benchmark to evaluate the influence of the informative prior.To systematically assess the impact of prior assumptions, we conduct a sensitivity analysis along three dimensions: Varying prior means: For the informative prior, we consider alternative hyperparameter combinations that shift the prior mean while keeping the variance constant. Specifically, we examine gamma priors with means of 1.5 and 2.5 by adjusting the shape and rate parameters accordingly (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1.5, b=1$$\end{document} for mean 1.5; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=2.5, b=1$$\end{document} for mean 2.5).Varying prior variances: We assess the influence of prior precision by fixing the prior mean at 2 and varying the variance. We consider moderate variance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gamma}(4,2)$$\end{document} with variance 1) and larger variance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gamma}(1,0.5)$$\end{document} with variance 4) to examine how posterior estimates respond to changes in prior uncertainty.Alternative prior families: To ensure that our conclusions are not an artifact of the gamma family, we also implemented a robustness check using independent log-normal priors for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document} (with parameters chosen to match the means and variances of the gamma priors). The results, available from the authors upon request, were virtually identical to those obtained with gamma priors, confirming that the choice of the gamma family does not drive our findings.

Bayesian with informative priors (B.Inf): We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \text {Gamma}(2,1)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \sim \text {Gamma}(2,1)$$\end{document}. These priors have a mean of 2 and a variance of 2, providing a weak but proper prior belief centered around a reasonable positive value. This choice reflects a modest degree of prior information without dominating the likelihood.

Bayesian with informative priors (B.Inf): We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \text {Gamma}(2,1)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \sim \text {Gamma}(2,1)$$\end{document}. These priors have a mean of 2 and a variance of 2, providing a weak but proper prior belief centered around a reasonable positive value. This choice reflects a modest degree of prior information without dominating the likelihood.

Bayesian with non- informative priors (B.Non-Inf): We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \text {Gamma}(0.001, 0.001)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \sim \text {Gamma}(0.001,$$\end{document} 0.001). This approximates an improper prior with mean 1 and a very large variance (1000), allowing the data to dominate the posterior. It serves as a benchmark to evaluate the influence of the informative prior.

Bayesian with non- informative priors (B.Non-Inf): We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sim \text {Gamma}(0.001, 0.001)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \sim \text {Gamma}(0.001,$$\end{document} 0.001). This approximates an improper prior with mean 1 and a very large variance (1000), allowing the data to dominate the posterior. It serves as a benchmark to evaluate the influence of the informative prior.

Varying prior means: For the informative prior, we consider alternative hyperparameter combinations that shift the prior mean while keeping the variance constant. Specifically, we examine gamma priors with means of 1.5 and 2.5 by adjusting the shape and rate parameters accordingly (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1.5, b=1$$\end{document} for mean 1.5; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=2.5, b=1$$\end{document} for mean 2.5).

Varying prior means: For the informative prior, we consider alternative hyperparameter combinations that shift the prior mean while keeping the variance constant. Specifically, we examine gamma priors with means of 1.5 and 2.5 by adjusting the shape and rate parameters accordingly (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1.5, b=1$$\end{document} for mean 1.5; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=2.5, b=1$$\end{document} for mean 2.5).

Varying prior variances: We assess the influence of prior precision by fixing the prior mean at 2 and varying the variance. We consider moderate variance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gamma}(4,2)$$\end{document} with variance 1) and larger variance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gamma}(1,0.5)$$\end{document} with variance 4) to examine how posterior estimates respond to changes in prior uncertainty.

Varying prior variances: We assess the influence of prior precision by fixing the prior mean at 2 and varying the variance. We consider moderate variance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gamma}(4,2)$$\end{document} with variance 1) and larger variance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gamma}(1,0.5)$$\end{document} with variance 4) to examine how posterior estimates respond to changes in prior uncertainty.

The results, available from the authors upon request, were virtually identical to those obtained with gamma priors, confirming that the choice of the gamma family does not drive our findings.

Asymptotic confidence intervals

Based on the asymptotic normality of the MLE, the FIM is commonly used to construct asymptotic CIs for the unknown parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi = (\alpha , \lambda ).$$\end{document} Under regularity conditions, the MLEs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\varphi } = (\tilde{\alpha }, \tilde{\lambda })$$\end{document} are asymptotically normally distributed. As the sample size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}, the distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\varphi }$$\end{document} converges to a bivariate normal distribution with mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi$$\end{document} and covariance matrix equal to the inverse of the FIM, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\!\!I^{-1}(\varphi ).$$\end{document} The FIM is defined as the negative expectation of the second-order derivatives of the log-likelihood function }\right) ^{\alpha }\left( 1\!-\!\frac{1}{2}e^{-\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) }{\left( \frac{3}{2}e^{-2\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\!-\!\frac{1}{2}e^{-3\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }}\right) }+\frac{2}{\lambda }\sum _{j=1}^{n}\left( \frac{y_{j}}{\lambda }\right) ^{\alpha }\left( 1+\alpha \ln \left(
